[{"id": "6B.6", "text": "Did preparedness laws, emergency decisions, funding, and implementation reduce avoidable and unequally distributed suffering during pandemics, disasters, displacement, famine, or economic shocks?"}, {"id": "6B.7", "text": "Did formal eligibility rules, administrative practice, and appeal systems prevent welfare, permits, jobs, food, and housing from becoming instruments of political loyalty or punishment?"}]

ANSWERS:
[{"question_id": "6B.6", "answer": "For 2023, crisis protection was mixed and its distributional performance was weak. The López Obrador government had formal disaster-financing and risk-transfer provisions adopted in 2021, but the World Bank noted that implementation and distributional risks remained, while the 2022 budget covered only an average or below-average loss year and could be exceeded by a major event; the IMF likewise reported that fiscal reserves had fallen below 0.1% of GDP and that proposed 2023-budget reforms had not yet been shown to operate. IMSS-BIENESTAR rules provided a federal health-service framework for people without social security, including preventive, hospital, epidemiological, community, and migrant services, but described authorized coverage rather than effective delivery. During Otis, federal authorities deployed troops, workers, and air and naval bridges, and government, state, municipal, UN, civil-society, and private actors coordinated shelter, health, nutrition, water, communications, and reconstruction efforts. These actions demonstrate mobilization, but not that suffering was adequately or equitably reduced: early reporting described delayed aid, shortages of food, water, fuel, medicine, and communications, with poor residents receiving little assistance. The El Bosque displacement response provides direct evidence of unmet basic needs: reported mistreatment and a full day without food or water in a temporary shelter, stalled promised relocation, homelessness or return to an uninhabitable area, and inadequate water, health, electricity, and education services. Earlier disaster evidence also describes reactive rather than preventive administration, weak coordination and follow-up, insufficient disaggregated impact data, and particular exposure of poor, Indigenous, rural, and otherwise marginalized communities. Attribution is strongest for federal laws, budget arrangements, troop and resource deployment, and AMLO’s crisis decisions, but service delivery and outcomes were shared with states, municipalities, the military, UN agencies, civil society, private actors, and donors; Otis’s exceptional rapid intensification also limits causal claims. The evidence therefore supports some preparedness and substantial emergency mobilization, alongside significant avoidable and unequally distributed suffering, without establishing final recovery outcomes or a full pandemic, famine, displacement, or economic-shock counterfactual.", "supporting_evidence_ids": ["BATCH-0014-E016", "BATCH-0051-E002", "BATCH-0003-E022", "BATCH-0015-E015", "BATCH-0015-E012", "BATCH-0015-E017", "BATCH-0029-E003"], "contrary_or_qualifying_evidence_ids": ["BATCH-0017-E003", "BATCH-0002-E010", "BATCH-0015-E011", "BATCH-0031-E003", "BATCH-0018-E007", "BATCH-0044-E009", "BATCH-0050-E010", "BATCH-0053-E007"], "limitations_and_gaps": ["BATCH-0002-E010 concerns 2019–2021 preparedness activities and does not establish López Obrador-specific 2023 implementation or closure of identified findings.", "BATCH-0014-E016 and BATCH-0017-E003 describe legal or proposed financial mechanisms and preparedness expectations, not verified 2023 operation or realized outcomes.", "UN-supported shelter, education, health, displacement, and Otis outputs cannot be attributed solely to the federal government or to López Obrador.", "Otis evidence is largely an early-response snapshot and does not establish final recovery, mortality, or distributional outcomes.", "El Bosque evidence includes community reports received by Amnesty and does not establish the final federal response after publication.", "The supplied record does not provide a complete pandemic, famine, displacement, or economic-shock counterfactual or a systematic 2023 national comparison.", "BATCH-0053-E007 supplies a 2022 baseline of severe deprivation and health-access problems but does not establish whether conditions improved or worsened during 2023."], "corrections_made": ["Removed the implication that 2019–2022 exercises were direct 2023 evidence; The 2023 IMSS-BIENESTAR rules required free, equal, confidential, non-discriminatory care, complaint channels, citizen participation, supervision, and corrective action; Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro prohibited political-electoral proselytism in training. CONEVAL and other monitoring arrangements also supplied formal measurement mechanisms. However, implementation was uneven: evaluations found unclear targeting, weak administrative capacity, incomplete indicators, and limited replicability; Sembrando Vida rules did not clearly define selection or prioritization, while reported access and payment irregularities burdened Indigenous and rural beneficiaries. Evidence also describes direct transfers as potentially clientelist and reports a shift in social spending away from the poorest groups, although it does not prove that every allocation was partisan or personally ordered by López Obrador. Administrative and human-rights safeguards were likewise incomplete: CNDH documented 2023 migration cases involving due-process, non-refoulement, dignity, and liberty violations, and the UN Working Group found detention safeguards and amparo remedies often ineffective, including punishment or threatened prolonged detention associated with exercising legal rights. These findings support mixed protection against politicized or punitive allocation, with stronger formal rules than demonstrated practice. Direct evidence is limited for political allocation of housing, permits, and ordinary public jobs, and the cited migration violations are attributable primarily to implementing authorities rather than personally to the president.", "supporting_evidence_ids": ["BATCH-0051-E003", "BATCH-0051-E004", "BATCH-0049-E001", "BATCH-0038-E005", "BATCH-0031-E019", "BATCH-0015-E004", "BATCH-0016-E003"], "contrary_or_qualifying_evidence_ids": ["BATCH-0018-E006", "BATCH-0050-E002", "BATCH-0031-E015", "BATCH-0015-E005", "BATCH-0021-R01-E014", "BATCH-0049-E011", "BATCH-0013-E009"], "limitations_and_gaps": ["The evidence is strongest for social transfers, health care, and migration administration; This information is\n               delivered to about 4,000 households.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. The Information Centre\n               makes the invitation and provides transportation for the community groups. This programme receives an\n               average of 300 people per year.\n        c)     Visits to schools around LVNPS. Based on these conventions, there is an\n        established procedure to declare a radiological emergency at a Mexican nuclear\n        installation if an event has the likelihood of affecting neighbouring states. The\n        procedure’s objective, to provide early notification, and its application are the\n        responsibility of CNSNS. Notwithstanding the preceding, Mexico has bilateral\n        agreements for exchanging technical information (including the occurrence of\n        significant events) with their closest neighbours.\n\n        Mexico is also part of the IAEA Early Communications International System, which\n        would be used to immediately provide information to the IAEA in the event of a\n        radiological emergency at LVNPS. The same information would be available to\n        neighbouring states. Mexico is also a signatory of the “Vienna Convention on Civil\n        Liability for Nuclear Damage”. It is important to mention that the LVNPS site is over\n        500 km away from the border of the nearest country.\n\n        Mexico has a National System for Civil Protection. In the event of a radiological\n        emergency at a nuclear installation in Unites States of America, near the border with\n        Mexico, with the likelihood to affect the national territory, the DN-III Plan would be\n        activated. The Ministry of Defence is responsible to apply the DN-III Plan under the\n        direction of the Secretariat of the Interior, assisted by the Secretariat of Health and\n        DICONSA. The DN-III Plan includes the infrastructure required to establish adequate\n        communications, evacuation capacity and establishment of control points for taking\n        the appropriate actions during the emergency. Based on this assumption, the LVNPS developed strategies in which\n                     the response capacity is not compromised by the failure of any common\n                     system and/or equipment.\n\n              2)    Additional responsibilities of government agencies\n\n                     As mentioned before in this Article 16, with regard to the External\n                     Radiological Emergency Plan, the Organization for Emergencies Response\n                     is formed by the federal and state agencies listed previously, which form the\n                     External Radiological Emergency Plan Committee.\n\n              3)    New procedures\n\n                     LVNPS has developed the Severe Accident Management Guidelines (SAMG)\n                     based on the guidelines for the Emergency Procedures and for Severe\n                     Accidents EPG / SAG (Emergency Procedure Guidelines / Severe Accident\n                     Guidelines) Rev. 3 of the BWR Reactor Owners Group.
